Both conjuncts matter: the flag alone would placeholder even when OneCLI is unconfigured (proxy never applied → `key=onecli-managed` on a DIRECT request → `REQUEST_DENIED`), and `isOneCliConfigured()` alone is the pre-#640 prod state (agents proxy-less). With either off (dev, or prod pre-cutover) the real value is forwarded via the existing `SECRET_CONTAINER_VARS` env-file as before. The remaining gap — the gate passes but `applyOneCliToSpawn()` returns false because the gateway is unreachable — is closed by **failing the spawn closed**: when managed placeholders were applied but the proxy could not be, `runContainerAgent` cleans up the secret env-file and throws an actionable error rather than launching a container whose withheld credentials would fail on direct requests. The queue retries with backoff, so a transient gateway blip self-heals; untrusted spawns forward no vars, so nothing is placeholdered and they proceed unaffected. `applyOneCliToSpawn` (`src/onecli-client.ts`) now also injects `NO_PROXY=host.docker.internal,localhost,127.0.0.1` whenever the gateway proxy env lands on a spawn (keyed on the actual presence of `HTTP_PROXY`/`HTTPS_PROXY` in the argv, not on the SDK's return value, so future SDK drift can't silently resurrect the incident below): the gateway supplies `HTTP_PROXY`+`HTTPS_PROXY` but no `NO_PROXY`, and the agent reaches its credential-proxy over plain HTTP at `http://host.docker.internal:3001` (`ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL`) — without the exclusion that hop routes through the gateway and ECONNRESETs the LLM path (INCIDENT-746). The swap was probe-verified host-side on 2026-07-09: a placeholder-key Distance Matrix request through the gateway returned real distances (Nashville→Memphis, 342 km), and the vaulted GitHub PAT that co-activates under the same flag was pre-flighted valid + repo-scoped. Inert until an operator sets `ONECLI_AGENT_PROXY=1` on the NAS. Coverage: spawn-path tests assert the placeholder lands only when the agent proxy is enabled (real value never reaches argv), that a configured-but-proxy-off prod state keeps the real-value path, that an unreachable gateway fails the spawn closed (no container launched), and that `NO_PROXY` is injected whenever proxy env is present on the argv.

Nothing used it. ## [1.2.62] - 2026-07-07

- Fixed final-response reply-threading getting clobbered by mid-turn piped inbound (#722). The per-chat `pendingReplyTo` anchor is set to the triggering message at turn start and consumed on the first user-visible reply — but the pipe path overwrote it unconditionally, so a turn that ran long enough to absorb new messages threaded its final answer to whatever landed last instead of the question it was answering (live repro: two consecutive wrong-quotes in the main chat on 2026-07-07, "ты не на то ответил"). A pipe now claims the anchor only when it is free (new pure `claimReplyAnchor` in `src/agent-output-action.ts`): an in-flight turn keeps its quote, while the post-consume case — where a later output genuinely answers the piped follow-up — still claims correctly. Piped messages are processed exactly as before; only the quoting anchor is protected. The anchor is also consumed at the IPC visible-send boundary (`send_message`/`send_file` with confirmed delivery, own-chat only) — the mark-displayed consumption arrives only with the SDK result, and a pipe landing in that gap must be able to claim the anchor for the follow-up turn (review round 2). The pipe-path debug log now records `anchorClaimed`.

## [1.2.61] - 2026-07-06

- Added a deterministic host-side work-evidence post-check for cadence-driven scheduled tasks (#720). Reference incident: a maintenance agent on a pinned SDK session fabricated an evidence-gated skill's success report — it claimed `verification: "live"` without ever writing the state file, so `task_run_logs` recorded `success` while the work never happened. A skill can now declare `evidence: "<relative-file>#<json-field>"` in its cadence frontmatter (e.g. `cfp-state.json#_last_checked`); the cadence registry validates the shape (exactly one `#`, both halves non-empty, file half relative with no `..` traversal) and threads it into a new `scheduled_tasks.evidence` column (PRAGMA-gated migration; evidence changes count as declaration shape changes on rebuild; owner-scheduled tasks leave it NULL). After a run that would otherwise be `success`, `runTask` reads `groups/<group_folder>/<file>` as JSON and requires the named top-level string field to parse as a date >= the run's start time — proof the artifact was freshened during the run. Any failure (missing file, invalid JSON, missing/non-string field, unparseable date, stale date) records the run as `error` with message prefix `evidence-check:` AND clears the task's pinned session (plus wipes its now-orphan transcript per #193 hygiene), because a resumed session carries the fabricated report as in-context precedent and keeps fabricating, while a fresh session was experimentally shown to run honestly. `error`/`killed`/`precheck_skipped` runs are never penalized — a precheck-gated no-op never woke the agent, so no evidence is expected. The check itself is a pure exported helper (`checkTaskEvidence` in `src/task-scheduler.ts`) unit-tested alongside scheduler-driven integration coverage.

## [1.2.60] - 2026-07-06

- Forwarded `SESSIONIZE_SPEAKER_KEY` and `SESSIONIZE_EVENT_API_KEY` into main/trusted agent containers via the secret env-file (#719, PR #714). The `jbaruch/nanoclaw-conferences` tile's deterministic check-cfps pipeline (`discover-open-cfps.py`, `verify-sessionize.py`) reads both directly from the container env, but neither was in `CONTAINER_VARS`, so since 2026-07-04 `nightly-cfp-sync` exited on "SESSIONIZE_SPEAKER_KEY is unset", skipped verification, and froze the `_last_checked` cursor — reported daily as `status=success` (the #601 silent-success watchdog surfaced it as `work_evidence_stale`). Both keys route through `SECRET_CONTAINER_VARS` (mode-0600 env-file, off `ps`/`docker ps`) mirroring the `YOUTUBE_API_KEY` pattern; untrusted containers never receive them. Coverage is outcome-level per review: a spawn-path test asserts the keys land in the materialized `--env-file` content on a main spawn (the exact regression shape — present in `.env`, absent from `CONTAINER_VARS`) and are absent from untrusted spawns and from visible `-e` argv. Root fix authored by AyeAye; test hardening + release bookkeeping added at review.

## [1.2.59] - 2026-07-05

- Documented the two runtime inference auth modes in `.env.example`: Mode A (`ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`, metered) and Mode B (`CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN` subscription OAuth — generated via `claude setup-token`, mutually exclusive with the API key, token counts still captured in `usage.jsonl`). The OAuth mode has existed in `src/credential-proxy.ts` since the proxy's two-mode design but was undocumented; this deployment now runs on it. Docs-only — no runtime change.

## [1.2.58] - 2026-07-05

- Decommissioned the LiteLLM gateway and removed the credential-proxy bypass machinery, completing the subscription-OAuth cutover's infrastructure cleanup. With OAuth auth the gateway is out of the request path entirely (the OAuth key-exchange endpoint doesn't exist on it), and 72h of gateway logs showed the `www.litellm.ai` cloud route was never selected — LiteLLM was running as a dead passthrough that `deploy.sh` step 8 kept self-healing back up. Deleted: `container/litellm/` (compose + router config), `scripts/ensure-gateway-up.sh` + its test (and the `ci.yml` step that invoked that test by module name — dropped in lock-step), deploy step 8, and `src/anthropic-bypass.ts` + test (the LiteLLM→anthropic-direct fallback decision logic; its second consumer, the Stage-2 classifier, was removed in 1.2.57). `src/credential-proxy.ts` loses the whole bypass path — the 5xx retry, the 3s idle-timeout teardown, the reachability re-dial, and the `LITELLM_MASTER_KEY` per-target key selection — and now forwards straight to `api.anthropic.com` (a test-only `upstreamUrl` option keeps the proxy suite off the real API). The `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL` host-side custom-endpoint override is retired with it, by operator decision — the third-party-endpoint options it served (Ollama proxy, Together, Fireworks) are dead, the deployment standardized on Anthropic models throughout — and the README's "third-party or open-source models" section (all three languages) is removed so the docs no longer advertise a capability the proxy doesn't have. Containers still receive `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL` pointing at the credential proxy — that container-facing role is unrelated and unchanged. The infra-container orphan-reap exemptions (`INFRA_CONTAINER_RE` in `src/container-runtime.ts`, the awk negation in `scripts/exclude-infra-containers.sh`) revert to plain `^nanoclaw-` matching — there is no infra container left to protect. `LITELLM_GATEWAY_API_KEY`, `LITELLM_MASTER_KEY`, and `ANTHROPIC_BYPASS_URL` are fully unreferenced and leave `.env.example`; `docker-compose.yml` keeps `extra_hosts: host.docker.internal` (OneCLI needs it) with the comment corrected. **Operator action:** stop and deregister the `nanoclaw-litellm` UGOS Pro project (symlink + compose-table row) — the deploy no longer resurrects it.

## [1.2.57] - 2026-07-04

- Removed the Stage-2 Haiku spam/intent classifier subsystem in full, now that the deployment runs on subscription-OAuth auth. The classifier (`src/gates/haiku-classifier.ts`) read `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` directly and had no OAuth path, so after the subscription cutover it self-disabled (fail-open to `pass`) and logged an ERROR per message — dead weight plus per-message log spam. Removal is behavior-neutral versus the current runtime (the gate already always passed); Stage-1 trigger matching is unchanged and remains the sole spawn gate. Deleted the gate and its registration (`src/gates/index.ts`); the `stage2Enabled`/`stage2ModelId`/`stage2ContextStrategy` `ContainerConfig` fields and their new-group defaulting (`src/types.ts`, `src/index.ts` — the whole `applyNewGroupContainerConfigDefaults` helper is gone); the `--set-stage2-enabled` mode of `scripts/update-trigger-patterns.ts` (trigger-keyword append mode retained); the context-strategy registry and `static-group-context` strategy (`src/gates/context-strategy.ts`, `src/gates/strategies/`), whose only caller was the classifier; and the `mineHaikuSamples` trigger-learner input with its `haiku_verdict` `TruthSource` (`src/gates/trigger-learner-runtime.ts`, `trigger-learner.ts`) — it fed on `haiku classifier verdict` host-logs no longer produced, and the learner keeps its `user_reaction`/`owner_correction` sources. The now-orphaned `ASSISTANT_OWNER_NAME` config export (the deleted strategy was its sole consumer) is removed; `ASSISTANT_OWNER_HANDLE` stays (still read by the learner). The `hydrateRegisteredGroupTriggerPatterns` (#670) regression test survives the removal of `src/register-group.test.ts`'s stage2 cases. The offline trigger-keyword seeding tools (`scripts/seed-trigger-keywords.ts`, `scripts/extract-keyword-candidates.ts`) carry their own inline Haiku labeling independent of the runtime gate and are retained. The credential-proxy bypass strip and LiteLLM decommission ship separately.

## [1.2.56] - 2026-07-02

- Rotate a recurring task's pinned SDK session when plugin content changes, so skill fixes actually load (#710). Per-task session reuse (#336) pins `scheduled_tasks.session_id` across cadence fires to keep the prompt-cache prefix warm, but skill/rule content is injected into the SDK session only at creation — the per-spawn `cpSync` snapshot was fresh on disk while the resumed session never re-read it, so a pinned cadence session silently outlived every plugin update (reference incident: the drive-planner notification fix shipped in nanoclaw-travel 0.2.4 never took effect because the `cadence-registry::telegram_swarm::tessl__drive-planner` session predated it, and the agent kept following week-old instructions plus its own in-context precedent). Fix: `setTaskSessionId` now stamps a `session_plugins_hash` column (new `src/plugin-content-hash.ts`, sha256 over the whole tessl registry tree resolved via `getRegistryTilesDir()`) in the same UPDATE as the id, and `runTask` compares it against the current registry hash at fire time — on mismatch it clears the pin, queues the stale transcript for the post-run wipe, and starts a fresh session that loads current content. The whole registry is hashed rather than just the task's named skill because a session's stale surface spans rules, cross-skill `Skill()` deps, and scripts — under-invalidation is the #710 failure mode, and the cost of over-rotation is one lost cache prefix per task per plugin update. NULL-vs-NULL compares as a match so registry-less installs keep resuming; a NULL stored hash under a live registry (every pre-#710 pinned row) mismatches on its first post-deploy fire and rotates exactly the stale sessions the issue describes. All `session_id` clear paths (`clearTaskSessionId`, `clearTaskSessionIdsForGroup`) clear the hash in lock-step.

## [1.2.55] - 2026-06-30

- Stopped the orchestrator's startup orphan-cleanup from SIGKILLing the `nanoclaw-litellm` gateway sidecar on every restart. `cleanupOrphans` (`src/container-runtime.ts`) runs at boot, lists `docker ps`, and stops everything whose name starts with `nanoclaw-` that isn't a graceful-shutdown handoff — but the LiteLLM sidecar's UGOS compose project names its container `nanoclaw-litellm-nanoclaw-litellm-1`, which matched that prefix and got killed (exit 137) on every orchestrator recreate, with NO deploy.sh involvement. Because an external `docker kill` suppresses `restart: always`, the gateway then stayed down until a manual `docker compose up -d`, silently degrading the credential path (the gateway bakes `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` at create time, so a fresh container is also required to pick up a rotated key). Observed during an `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` rotation: force-recreating the orchestrator to refresh the in-memory proxy key killed the just-recreated gateway. The infra-exclusion the deploy-side path already uses lived only in `scripts/exclude-infra-containers.sh` (the awk `^nanoclaw-litellm-nanoclaw-litellm-[0-9]+$` negation), not in the orchestrator. Fix: mirror that exact predicate in `cleanupOrphans` via an `INFRA_CONTAINER_RE` / `isInfraContainer(name)` check AND-ed into the orphan filter — matching the gateway's full `project+service+index` compose container name (index left flexible for a recreate/scale bump), so it excludes the gateway without exempting a per-group agent whose slug merely starts with `litellm` (e.g. `nanoclaw-litellm-fans-<ts>`). Lock-step with the shell predicate: if a second service is added to the gateway's compose project, extend both. Tests (`container-runtime.test.ts`): the gateway is never stopped (with an agent orphan present, and as the only running container), AND a `litellm`-slugged agent container is still reaped. Refs #609.

- Forwarded `TOMTOM_API_KEY` into main/trusted agent containers so the `jbaruch/nanoclaw-travel` tile's TomTom routing works — `maps_client.py` uses TomTom geocode + `calculateRoute` (`api.tomtom.com`) as the backup behind Google Maps, and the new `drive-planner` skill routes through it, but the key was never forwarded so TomTom stayed dark. Added to both `src/container-runner.ts` allowlists mirroring `GOOGLE_MAPS_API_KEY` — `CONTAINER_VARS` (forwarded into main/trusted spawns) and `SECRET_CONTAINER_VARS` (routed via the mode-0600 env-file so it stays off `docker ps`); documented in `.env.example`. **Operator action:** set `TOMTOM_API_KEY` in `~/nanoclaw/.env` on the NAS and redeploy (`./scripts/deploy.sh`) for the orchestrator to pick it up. Also corrected `docs/SECURITY.md` §4 and `docs/OPERATIONS.md`, which still described Composio as the only forwarded credential — stale since `GITHUB_TOKEN`/`BYAIR_MCP_URL`/`GOOGLE_MAPS_API_KEY`/`YOUTUBE_API_KEY` were added — to enumerate the actual forwarded set and point at `CONTAINER_VARS`/`SECRET_CONTAINER_VARS` as authoritative.
- Added a `persist_global_file` MCP tool + host-side IPC handler so approved soul-searching edits survive deploys (jbaruch/nanoclaw-admin#393). Root cause: a main container edits `/workspace/global/SOUL.md`, which is an RW bind onto the host's git-tracked `groups/global/SOUL.md` — but the container can't commit it (the `.git` is only reachable through the read-only `/workspace/project` mount, and push credentials are `/dev/null`-shadowed via `SECRET_FILES`). The edit lands only in the working tree, so `scripts/deploy.sh`'s `git stash; git pull` discarded it on the next deploy; every approved persona self-improvement was silently lost (recurring since ~2026-06-07). The new IPC handler (`src/ipc.ts`, `case 'persist_global_file'`) commits the working-tree change for an allowlisted set of persona files and pushes `HEAD:main` so the next `git pull` keeps it — modeled on `github_backup`'s push-auth wiring (`GITHUB_TOKEN` via `GIT_CONFIG_*` insteadOf), `isMain`-gated identically (a non-main container can't reach the persona source even by writing an IPC task file directly). The allowlist is the security boundary: a new pure, exported `validateGlobalFilesToPersist(files)` in `src/ipc.ts` accepts ONLY the exact basenames in `PERSISTABLE_GLOBAL_FILES` (`SOUL.md`, `SOUL-untrusted.md`) — never a caller-supplied path component — so a compromised container can't commit an arbitrary tracked file via traversal (`../../.env`), substitution (`CLAUDE.md`), or a subdir escape (`prompts/main.md`); an empty/absent payload defaults to both persona files, repeats dedupe, non-string entries are rejected stringified. `git add -- <allowlisted paths>` stages ONLY those paths so unrelated working-tree changes never ride along. A push failure surfaces as a `stage: 'git'` error envelope, with git stderr run through a new exported `redactGitToken` so a token the `insteadOf` rewrite embeds in the push URL (`x-access-token:<TOKEN>@github.com`) can't leak into the envelope or logs (`coding-policy: no-secrets`). The op never strands a committed-but-unpushed change (a silent non-retryable state): the side-effecting git boundary is an exported `persistGlobalFilesToGit({repoRoot, relPaths, message, token})` that runs git as direct `execFile` argv (no shell — the commit message can't inject), commits only when the allowlisted paths actually changed, and on a push failure after its own commit rolls it back (`reset --soft`, keeping the edit staged) so a later run retries cleanly; a no-edit run whose `HEAD` is already ahead of `origin/main` pushes that pending commit (recovers a strand from a prior failure). Because the push targets the protected `main` branch, it runs under the `coding-policy: ci-safety` Content-Only Direct-Push Carve-Out — the authority-of-record rule is `jbaruch/nanoclaw-host: persona-persist-direct-push`, and the deterministic push-time gate is enforced here: before pushing, `persistGlobalFilesToGit` enumerates every path the push would change on `main` (`git diff --name-only origin/main..HEAD`) and refuses outright (no push, rollback if it had committed) unless ALL are in `PERSISTABLE_GLOBAL_FILES` — so a recovery push of a HEAD we didn't author can never land a non-persona path on `main`. It's unit-tested directly against a throwaway repo + bare remote — commit-and-push, no-op, only-named-paths-staged, push-failure-rolls-back-and-stays-retryable, retry-lands-after-remote-recovers, stranded-commit-recovery, push-gate-refuses-a-non-allowlisted-pending-path, and the token-redacted failure envelope — keeping the inline IPC handler thin. Exposed to container agents as the `persist_global_file` MCP tool (`container/agent-runner/src/ipc-mcp-stdio.ts`, main-only registration, zod enum mirroring the handler allowlist as defense-in-depth) and classified through `confirmation-tokens.ts` as a new `persist_global_file` destructive scope: operator-driven applies (a trusted chain — Baruch replying "apply 1, 3") pass freely, but an untrusted-provenance attempt to rewrite the persona and push it to the deploy source is token-gated. Tests: `ipc.test.ts` (the allowlist validator's default/subset/dedupe/traversal/non-allowlisted/subdir-escape/non-string/non-array matrix, `redactGitToken`, and the `persistGlobalFilesToGit` git integration), `confirmation-tokens.test.ts` (the new scope classifies). The `soul-searching-apply` skill change to call this tool + add a post-write re-read verify ships separately in `jbaruch/nanoclaw-admin`. Refs jbaruch/nanoclaw-admin#393.

- Added per-group session-length cap overrides so a quiet group can run a tighter reset cap than the global default (#561). The session-length cap (#413) resets a session once it crosses a cumulative turn count (`SESSION_TURN_CAP`) or input-token total (`SESSION_TOKEN_CAP`), but those are a single global knob — and the global must cover the busiest group, so it can't be lowered without guillotining a group that legitimately runs long (observed: `telegram_old-wtf` reaches 81 turns in a real 2.4h conversation while three other groups peak at 1–10). The `session-cap-monitor` dashboard had spent a month proposing per-group reductions that were structurally inapplicable to a global knob, so none were ever acted on. `ContainerConfig` now carries optional `sessionTurnCap` / `sessionTokenCap` fields (mirroring the #395 `agentModel` / #509 `maintenanceAgentModel` per-group override pattern); a new pure `resolveSessionCaps(containerConfig, globalDefaults)` in `src/session-length-cap.ts` resolves the effective caps (a positive finite override wins; undefined/null/non-finite/non-positive/non-number inherits the global). `src/index.ts`'s per-turn reset check resolves via `resolveLiveSessionCaps(registeredGroups, folder, capturedConfig, globalDefaults)` — reading the **live** registry entry by folder rather than the `group` object captured at container spawn — so a `set_session_caps` IPC that lands mid-session takes effect on an already-active container's **next turn**, not only on its next spawn (it falls back to the captured config if the live entry is gone, e.g. group unregistered mid-run). Disabling a cap stays a global-only operation (global `<= 0`): a fat-fingered per-group `0`/negative/fractional falls back to the global rather than silently removing a group's bound, matching `resolvePerGroupAgentModel`'s invalid-falls-back contract. Settable via a new `set_session_caps` IPC handler (`src/ipc.ts`, owner-of-bill auth identical to `set_agent_model` — main targets any group, non-main only its own folder; sibling `containerConfig` fields preserved verbatim; each cap independently set / cleared-with-`null` / left-unchanged-when-omitted; non-positive **and non-integer** rejected so the raw-IPC path can't persist a fractional cap the MCP `.int()` schema would block) exposed to container agents as the `set_session_caps` MCP tool (`container/agent-runner/src/ipc-mcp-stdio.ts`), gated through `confirmation-tokens.ts` as a new `set_session_caps` destructive scope (allowed on operator-trusted chains, token-gated on untrusted-provenance). The tool's payload-building, empty-update guard (both caps omitted → actionable error, no IPC written), and response text are extracted to a pure `container/agent-runner/src/session-caps-payload.ts` (mirroring `agent-model-payload.ts`) so the bridge logic is unit-tested without standing up the MCP server. Tests: `session-length-cap.test.ts` (resolver fallback matrix, end-to-end through a reset verdict, and the live-registry mid-session no-respawn outcome), `ipc-auth.test.ts` (set/clear/partial-update/auth/sibling-preservation/non-positive+fractional rejection), `session-caps-payload.test.ts` (empty-update guard + payload shape + response text), `confirmation-tokens.test.ts` (the new scope classifies and gates). `.env.example` documents the per-group override beneath the global caps. The `session-cap-monitor` change to emit per-group recommendations that call this surface ships separately in `jbaruch/nanoclaw-admin`. Refs #413, #395, #509, #561.

- Stopped attaching the general-purpose subagent definitions on maintenance-session spawns, cutting the dominant cold-cache cost of recurring cadence tasks. The agent-runner builds a `general-purpose` subagent definition whose prompt embeds every installed skill name plus the full rule/behavior chain (~158K chars in production) and ships it as cached agent context on session creation — so it is re-created on every cold cache. Maintenance tasks (heartbeat, composio-fetch, morning-brief, nightly-*) fire on short cadences (heartbeat every 30 min) that always outlast the 5-min prompt-cache TTL, so each wake paid full cache-creation for that prompt — and a forensic pass over the audit window found **0 of 7869** maintenance runs ever called `Task`/`TeamCreate`, i.e. the definitions were never used. The cost was real: heartbeat alone ran ~27 wakes/day re-creating ~79–133K cache tokens each (~25M cache-creation tokens over 7 days), a meaningful slice of the maintenance API spend the #562 dashboard tracks. A new pure `shouldIncludeSubagentDefinitions(isMaintenanceSession, env)` in `container/agent-runner/src/subagent-prompt.ts` gates the whole surface: `container/agent-runner/src/index.ts` now skips the skill discovery, the rule/skill prompt assembly, AND the `agents` map (left empty `{}`) for maintenance spawns, while default/user-facing sessions are unchanged. A `MAINTENANCE_LOAD_SUBAGENTS=1` escape hatch force-includes the definitions if a future maintenance task genuinely needs to fan out to a subagent (documented in `.env.example` beside the #337 maintenance blocklists). Tests: `subagent-prompt.test.ts` (default-includes, maintenance-skips, exact-`1` opt-in only, non-maintenance ignores the flag). Deploy note: rebuilds the agent-runner image. Refs #560, #562.

- Upgraded `better-sqlite3` 11.10.0 → 12.10.0 and standardized the whole stack on Node.js 24 LTS. The 11.10.0 pin declared no `engines` and shipped no prebuilt binary for current Node — so when a local Homebrew bump pulled Node to a bleeding-edge major (26.x), the native addon failed to compile (`node-gyp` against V8 headers it predates) and every local test run died before a single assertion. The deployment was never affected (before this change the orchestrator and agent images pinned `node:22-slim`, where 11.10.0 builds fine), but the pin was a latent trap: any base-image bump would have reproduced the break in production. 12.10.0 declares `engines: 20.x || 22.x || 23.x || 24.x || 25.x || 26.x`, so it builds across every Node a contributor or image is likely to run; it's the newest release that clears the repo's `minReleaseAge=7d` supply-chain guard (12.10.1/12.11.1 were too fresh). The codebase only touches the stable `prepare`/`exec`/`pragma`/`transaction` surface, unchanged across the v11→v12 major. Alongside it, the drifted Node versions are unified on 24 LTS: `.nvmrc` 22 → 24, `ci.yml` `node-version` 20 → 24 (the paired gh-aw policy-review workflows already ran 24), and both `Dockerfile.orchestrator` and `container/Dockerfile` `node:22-slim` → `node:24-slim` (the `python:3.12-slim` audible-backup sidecar is unaffected). The Node-24 contract is carried through every surface so nothing still advertises the old floor: `package.json` `engines.node` `>=20.17` → `>=24`, `setup.sh`'s bootstrap gate `major >= 20` → `>= 24`, the `setup` skill's install path (`node@22`/`setup_22.x` → `node@24`/`setup_24.x`), and the `Node.js 20+`/`20以上` lines in `README.md`/`README_zh.md`/`README_ja.md`/`docs/SPEC.md` → `24+`. (`research/nas-migration-plan.md` keeps its `node:22-slim` example as a point-in-time design record.) Deploy note: this rebuilds and redeploys the orchestrator and agent-runner images on the new base. Full suite (3594 tests) green on Node 24 with 12.10.0.

- Normalized `messages.telegram_message_id` to hold the Telegram message ID for BOTH directions so `reply_to_message_id` has a single column to join against (#691). Pre-fix, inbound (user) rows stored the Telegram ID only in `id` (`telegram_message_id` NULL) while bot sends stored a synthetic `bot-<ts>-<rand>` in `id` and the Telegram ID in `telegram_message_id` — so the intuitive `WHERE id = :reply_to_message_id` silently returned zero rows whenever the parent was a bot-authored message (the common case of a user replying to one of the assistant's own messages), and the inverse `WHERE telegram_message_id = …` missed replies to inbound messages. The application code path was already safe (`getMessageById` does a two-pass `id`-then-`telegram_message_id` fallback), but any direct SQL consumer — notably the container agents querying `messages.db` — had no single join target and either silently lost replies-to-bot-messages or paid extra tool calls rediscovering the split at runtime. The Telegram channel inbound write path (`src/channels/telegram.ts`) now stamps `telegram_message_id` with the Telegram ID on inbound rows too (`id` still carries it as well), centralized in a `deliverInbound` helper so EVERY inbound path — text, photo, voice, document, `storeNonText` media — gets the stamp without per-handler drift (not just `message:text`). A PRAGMA-positioned, idempotent startup backfill (`src/db.ts`) copies `id` into `telegram_message_id` for existing `tg:%` rows that lack it, guarded by `id NOT LIKE 'bot-%'`: on a DB that predates the column, the ALTER leaves legacy bot sends NULL too, and their synthetic `bot-<ts>-<rand>` `id` (real Telegram ID never recorded, pre-#80) must NOT be copied into the Telegram-native ID column — those rows stay NULL. After this, `reply_to_message_id` resolves cleanly against `telegram_message_id` for every Telegram row regardless of direction, served by the existing `idx_messages_chat_telegram_id (chat_jid, telegram_message_id)` index; non-Telegram channels keep `telegram_message_id` NULL (their platform ID lives in `id`). The `messages`-table schema comment now documents `telegram_message_id` as the join contract. `getBotMessageByTelegramId`'s `is_bot_message = 1` filter keeps it bot-only despite inbound rows now populating the column. Tests: `db-migration.test.ts` (backfill copies inbound `id`→`telegram_message_id`, leaves bot rows, non-`tg:%` rows, AND legacy column-missing bot rows untouched/NULL, resolves a reply to both an inbound and a bot parent via the single column, idempotent on re-boot); `db.test.ts` (forward-path: a new inbound row and a bot row both resolve via the same `WHERE chat_jid = ? AND telegram_message_id = ?` join). Closes #691. Refs #80, #107.

- Stopped the recurring 1-in-7 `morning-brief` silent success — a run recorded `status=success` with an empty result that never reached chat (#689, reopening the #589/#682 lineage). Root cause: when the final compose-and-send turn drained without an SDK `result` event, the agent-runner's #461 silent-stop synthesis (and the post-query session-update) emitted a TERMINAL `{status:'success'}` marker. That marker carries no `streamText`, so it flipped the host's `hadTerminalResult` true — defeating #682's `!hadTerminalResult` killed-classification — and a brief that was composed but never sent was logged as a delivered success; zero alerting fired. (The issue blamed the in-container hard-exit watchdog, but #589-reopened already disabled that watchdog for maintenance sessions; the real masking was the synthesized success.) The fix is a declarative, skill-intent-aware signal rather than another timeout bump: a skill that always delivers user-facing content declares `requires_delivery: true` in its `SKILL.md` frontmatter (mirroring the existing per-skill `drain_timeout_ms` mechanism). When such a skill ends a `runQuery` without delivering anything — no successful `send_message`/`send_file` and no non-empty result text — the runner stamps the terminal marker with a new `noDelivery: true` field. The marker keeps `status:'success'` so the host's `scheduleClose` still drains the maintenance slot promptly (no #461 wedge regression), but the host latches `noDelivery` (so a trailing plain session-update success can't clear it) and resolves the run as `killed` (incomplete/retriable) in both the clean-exit and inactivity-timeout close paths — feeding the existing #682/#589 `killed`→retriable recovery path so the brief redelivers instead of vanishing. Skills that don't declare `requires_delivery` are unaffected: `noDelivery` is never stamped, so a legitimately-silent maintenance task (heartbeat, a precheck-gated cleanup that sends nothing) keeps its `success` — the declaration is the only thing distinguishing "drained without delivering, and that's a failure" from "drained without delivering, and that's fine", which the runner can't infer otherwise. New module `container/agent-runner/src/delivery-requirement.ts` (`parseRequiresDeliveryFromFrontmatter` + `resolveRequiresDelivery`, the safe-name guard and ENOENT/ENOTDIR fallback shared with `resolveDrainTimeoutMs`); `SAFE_SKILL_NAME_RE` is now exported from `hard-exit-watchdog.ts` for reuse. The `requires_delivery: true` declaration on `morning-brief` ships separately in `jbaruch/nanoclaw-admin` (additive/backward-compatible, so deploy order is unconstrained). Tests: `delivery-requirement.test.ts` (frontmatter parsing variants + resolver fixtures incl. path-traversal rejection); `container-runner.test.ts` (noDelivery marker → `killed`; latch survives a trailing plain success; no-flag terminal success stays `success`; default/interactive noDelivery stays `success` — maintenance-scoped). Closes #689. Refs #589, #682, #461, #685.

- Hardened `scripts/deploy.sh` step 8 (nanoclaw-litellm gateway self-heal) to verify the gateway STAYED up instead of fire-and-forget `docker compose up -d`. Observed 2026-06-16: during a deploy the freshly-started gateway was SIGKILLed ~1s later (exit 137, NOT OOM — the host had 3.6 GB free), a race against the concurrent orchestrator restart + agent-runner image rebuild. Because an external kill suppresses `restart: always`, the gateway then stayed down and the orchestrator silently bypassed to anthropic-direct (degraded: no LiteLLM cost tier-down) with only a stream of `Connection error` lines to show for it; a manual `docker compose up -d` brought it back cleanly. The verify-and-re-up loop now lives in a new `scripts/ensure-gateway-up.sh`: after `up -d` it confirms the named `nanoclaw-litellm` service's container reached Docker state `running` (`docker compose ps -q nanoclaw-litellm` → `docker inspect -f '{{.State.Status}}'`), re-ups up to 3 times with a 5s settle between checks, then surfaces a loud stderr `WARNING` naming the `docker compose logs` inspect command. The deploy is intentionally NOT failed on a still-down gateway — the anthropic-direct bypass keeps the system serving, so a hard abort would block on a non-critical, self-recoverable degradation. The retry control flow is extracted to its own script (per `coding-policy: script-delegation` + the `testing-standards` platform-bound carve-out) so it is CI-tested with `docker` stubbed on PATH — `scripts/test_ensure_gateway_up.py` (stdlib unittest, wired into `.github/workflows/ci.yml`) covers running-first-check, recover-after-one-failed-start, and never-running → non-fatal WARNING; only the genuinely-unhostable docker calls stay unhostable. The cadence is env-overridable (`GATEWAY_MAX_ATTEMPTS`, `GATEWAY_SETTLE_SECS`) so the test runs with zero sleep. Refs #609.

- Stopped cadence/maintenance containers crashing with an unhandled EPIPE during teardown and being mis-recorded as failed runs (#685). After the agent-runner streamed its terminal result marker and entered the post-query idle wait, the orchestrator tore the container down; a write to a socket the runner does NOT own (the Agent SDK's HTTP keep-alive connection, an MCP-server child pipe) then raised an async EPIPE `'error'` on a `Socket` with no listener → `Unhandled 'error' event` → `emitErrorCloseNT (node:internal/streams/destroy)` → the process died with a noisy stack and exit 1. Two weekly monitors (`session-cap-monitor`, `silent-success-watchdog`) hit the identical stack within 30 min on 2026-06-15. Two compounding gaps: the #560 `installStdioResilience` attaches `'error'` listeners only to `process.stdout`/`process.stderr`, so an EPIPE on any other socket escaped it; and the host's `container.on('close')` (`src/container-runner.ts`) records any non-zero exit as `status:'error'` regardless of the terminal result it already parsed off the stream, so a maintenance run that had fully delivered its verdict was logged as a failure (the captured 2026-06-15T10:45 run had already streamed `{status:'success',result:null}` before the crash). A new `installUncaughtEpipeGuard` (`container/agent-runner/src/stdio-resilience.ts`) installs a process-level `uncaughtException` handler scoped to EPIPE: it exits 0 when a terminal result was already delivered — the crash is post-delivery teardown noise, so the container exits cleanly and the host's #682 `code===0` + `hadTerminalResult` path records the run on its merits instead of as an error — and exits 1 otherwise (the output channel is gone before the run could deliver, a failed run mirroring the existing stdout-EPIPE policy, never a silent success). A non-EPIPE uncaught exception is a genuine defect and still rethrows so it crashes loudly (matching `makeStdioErrorHandler`). `writeOutput` flips a `deliveredTerminalResult` flag on any terminal marker — a `writeOutput` with no `streamText`, the same preview-vs-terminal discriminator the host uses for `hadTerminalResult`. The fix is agent-runner-side only; no host change is needed because a clean exit 0 routes through the existing #682 classification. The handler carries the `outer-boundary-process-contract` audit token per `coding-policy: error-handling`. Tests (`container/agent-runner/src/stdio-resilience.test.ts`): EPIPE-after-delivery → exit 0; EPIPE-before-delivery → exit 1; non-EPIPE → rethrow without exiting; and the installed guard routes an `uncaughtException` EPIPE through the handler per delivery state. The separate observability question the issue raised — why one of the two crashes surfaced a task-failure alert and the other was silent — is host-side alerting and is left to follow-up. Closes #685. Refs #560, #682, #589.

- Stopped scheduled/maintenance tasks leaking their terminal status text to the main chat as NULL-`telegram_message_id` ghost sends (#681). The task-scheduler streaming callback (`src/task-scheduler.ts`) auto-forwarded a scheduled run's terminal assistant text to chat whenever it wasn't fully wrapped in `<internal>` and the agent hadn't already delivered via `send_message` (`chat_displayed`). So heartbeat/monitor/brief runs that decided they had nothing to surface — and therefore never called `send_message` — still emitted their leftover status text (`Ready.`, a bare `✓`, `Cycle complete.`, an internal status dump, even a hallucinated "host-fix-required" bug report) straight into the owner's chat, model-agnostic (leaked on both the Sonnet and the #613 Haiku tiers, so prompt-level "stay silent" reinforcement was never going to close it). The forward is now gated on `taskSkill === undefined`: skill-invoking tasks — anything whose prompt carries a `Skill(skill: "tessl__…")` call, i.e. every maintenance/monitor/brief — surface ONLY via an explicit `send_message`, while raw reminders/one-shots (no skill call, where the terminal text IS the intended surface) keep forwarding. `task_run_logs.result` is still populated unconditionally, so silent-success accounting and forensic greps are unaffected. Second half: the remaining reminder forward now records `telegram_message_id` and gates its bot-row write on delivery via the shared `shouldStoreBotMessage` predicate — closing the NULL-`telegram_message_id` observability hole (pre-fix every scheduled-task send wrote a row with no native id, so a leak couldn't be traced back to a real Telegram send) and skipping the phantom row when Telegram swallows the send (matching the inbound #428 and IPC send paths). `shouldStoreBotMessage` is relocated from `src/ipc.ts` to `src/db.ts` — its true home next to `storeMessage`, and cycle-safe since `ipc.ts` imports `task-scheduler.ts` — so all bot-row write sites share one gate; `src/ipc.ts`, `src/index.ts`, and `src/ipc.test.ts` re-point their imports. `deps.sendMessage` on the task-scheduler now returns `string | void` (the channel-native id) instead of `void`. Tests (`src/task-scheduler.test.ts`): a skill-invoking task's terminal text is suppressed (no send, no bot row) while `task_run_logs.result` still captures it; a reminder forward records `telegram_message_id` when the channel returns one; a Telegram reminder forward that returns no id skips the bot row. Closes #681. Refs #81, #428, #581, #613.

- Stopped recording an incomplete maintenance run as `task_run_logs.status='success'` when the container exits cleanly (code 0) without ever delivering a terminal result (#682). The normal streaming-mode exit path in `src/container-runner.ts` resolved `{status:'success', result:null}` on ANY code-0 exit, with no check that the agent reached a terminal result — so the 2026-06-11 morning-brief, whose in-container hard-exit watchdog `process.exit(0)`'d after streaming the compose preview but before `send_message` fired, was logged `success` with an empty result, indistinguishable from a genuine delivery; zero alerting fired and the owner noticed ~3h later. (#683 closed the *host-timeout* shape — a maintenance container reaped by the `MAINTENANCE_CONTAINER_TIMEOUT` inactivity timer → `killed` — but the watchdog `process.exit(0)` is `timedOut=false`, so it landed in the non-timeout clean-exit path #683 left untouched, and #683's gate used `hadStreamingOutput`, which flips for streaming previews too and so can't tell "delivered a result" from "only previewed".) The fix adds the precise "did the run actually deliver a terminal result?" signal #683 deferred here: the host now tracks `hadTerminalResult`, set when a parsed output marker carries no `streamText` — the agent-runner emits a preview marker (the only `writeOutput` carrying `streamText`) on every throttled assistant-text snapshot, while every terminal marker (real result, empty-turn success, #461 silent-stop synthesis, error, precheck-skip) omits it. A MAINTENANCE container that exits code 0 with no terminal result is now resolved `killed` (incomplete, retriable) instead of `success`. The careful part — it does NOT mis-flag #461's legitimate silent-stop no-op successes: those emit a terminal marker (`{status:'success', result:''}`, no `streamText`), so `hadTerminalResult` is set and they stay `success`. The same signal also refines #589's timeout branch: a maintenance run that DID deliver a terminal result and then idled until the host reaped it now keeps idle-cleanup `success` (the work landed) instead of being over-reported as `killed`; only a reap after previews-but-no-terminal-result stays `killed`. Scoped to maintenance to mirror #683 — the `'killed'` status is consumed by `src/task-scheduler.ts` (which maps it to `task_run_logs.status='killed'` with the reason in the error column), and interactive idle-cleanup `success` semantics are unchanged. The scheduler `killed`-handling comment, fallback reason, and reclassification log are reworded source-agnostically (timeout-reap OR clean-exit), and the now-stale `ContainerOutput`/timeout-branch comments that said "precise terminal-result tracking is #682" are updated to describe the signal this change introduces. Tests (`src/container-runner.test.ts`): maintenance clean-exit-after-preview-only → `killed`; maintenance #461-silent-stop terminal marker + clean exit → `success` (the no-op guard); default/interactive clean-exit-after-preview-only → `success` (over-reach guard); maintenance delivered-then-idled-out → `success` (the #589 over-report refinement). The scheduler-side `killed`→`status='killed'` mapping is already covered by #683's test and is identical for both kill sources. The admin-tile follow-ups remain open: the detector backstop (extend `silent-success-watchdog` past its 10s gate, `jbaruch/nanoclaw-admin#364`) and Phase-C redelivery of a `killed` brief (`jbaruch/nanoclaw-admin#363`). Closes #682. Refs #589, #683, #461, #496.

- Added an explicit `claude-opus-4-8` row to `src/usage-log.ts` `PRICING`, resolving the cost-telemetry caveat #667 opened (#668). Since #667 bumped the default agent model to `claude-opus-4-8[1m]`, the 95+ main-tier records logged under model `claude-opus-4-8` (the API echoes the bare ID — the `[1m]` context tag never reaches the log) were priced via the #479 sub-#2 family-version fallback to `claude-opus-4-7` and flagged `cost_approximate: true`. Anthropic's published Opus 4.8 rates were confirmed against the live pricing page to be **identical** to 4.7 — input $5 / output $25 / MTok, cache-read $0.50, 5m-write $6.25, 1h-write $10 — so the prior fallback was already numerically correct (the issue's worry that 4.8 might be cheaper, over-stating cost, did not materialize); this change makes 4.8 records written henceforth exact instead of approximate. `usage.jsonl` is append-only, so the 95+ already-logged rows keep their `cost_approximate: true` flag — there is no retroactive rewrite, and none is needed since the numbers were already correct. The two tests that used `claude-opus-4-8` as their "unknown future Opus" stand-in (`resolvePricing` family-fallback and `parseUsageFromBody` `cost_approximate` flagging) are repointed to `claude-opus-4-9` so they still exercise the latest-in-family fallback (which now resolves to the new 4-8 row), and a new test asserts `claude-opus-4-8` is an exact match with no fallback flags. Stale `claude-opus-4-8` examples in the four surrounding doc comments are bumped to `claude-opus-4-9`, and the table's "as of" verification date is refreshed to 2026-06. Closes #668. Refs #667, #613.

- Fixed the `scheduled_reminders` JSON→DB migration (state-004, #296) looping on every orchestrator startup when a source row had a null/missing `reminder_offset_min` (#676). `migrateScheduledRemindersJsonFiles` (`src/db.ts`) bound each row's fields into the `reminder_offset_min INTEGER NOT NULL` column; a row whose value was null (observed in production) threw a NOT NULL `SqliteError` inside the per-file `db.transaction`, which rolled the WHOLE file back — so every good reminder in that file was lost, not just the bad row — and `handleConstraintViolationOrRethrow` `continue`d *before* `renameMigratedSource`, so the source JSON was never retired and the migration re-attempted (and re-logged the error) on every boot. The per-row guard only rejected non-object rows, not malformed-but-object rows. Now a new `missingRequiredScheduledReminderFields` helper returns the list of NOT NULL source fields a row is missing or mis-typing (`event_id`/`title`/`utc_time`/`task_id` must be strings, `reminder_offset_min` a finite number), and the migration warn-and-skips any row with a non-empty list — naming the offending field(s) in the warn for triage (`counts.skipped++`, like the existing non-object guard) — instead of letting it abort the transaction, so the good rows import, the source is renamed, and the startup loop ends. The constraint-class catch stays as a defensive net for any violation the per-row guard doesn't anticipate. The migration test that documented the old whole-file-rollback-leaves-source-in-place behaviour is replaced with a `#676` case: one file mixing two good rows with a null-`reminder_offset_min` row, a missing-`task_id` row, and a numeric (non-string) `event_id` row asserts the good rows import, each bad row skips with the correct `missing` field list, the `event_id` field is omitted from the warn when it isn't a string, and the source is retired. Closes #676.

- Gave the Stage 2 Haiku classifier the same LiteLLM→anthropic-direct bypass the agent containers ride, closing the egress-reachability asymmetry #671 observed (#675). The classifier (`src/gates/haiku-classifier.ts`) runs in the orchestrator process and built a single `Anthropic` client against `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL` (the `nanoclaw-litellm` gateway) with no fallback — so when the gateway was intermittently unreachable from the host, container haiku spawns sailed through (the credential proxy retries against `ANTHROPIC_BYPASS_URL`) while the classifier's direct call died on `Connection error`, which is exactly the outage #672 had to make *safe* with deterministic gating. The classifier now resolves a client *pair* (primary → `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL`, bypass → `ANTHROPIC_BYPASS_URL`) and, on a bypass-eligible primary failure, retries once against the bypass. Both clients route their endpoint through `parseAnthropicUrlOrDefault`, so a malformed `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL` / `ANTHROPIC_BYPASS_URL` degrades the affected client to anthropic-direct (with a value-omitted warn) rather than handing the SDK a bad baseURL — valid / unset values pass through unchanged. The bypass decision logic is extracted into a new `src/anthropic-bypass.ts` shared by both egress paths so the proxy (socket-level) and classifier (SDK-level) can't drift: `BYPASS_TRIGGER_STATUS_CODES` (5xx), `BYPASS_REACHABILITY_CODES` / `isReachabilityErrorCode` (ECONNREFUSED/ENOTFOUND/EHOSTUNREACH/ECONNRESET), `resolveBypassTarget` (resolves both endpoint env vars through `parseAnthropicUrlOrDefault` — `URL.canParse`, no throw — and judges enablement on the RESOLVED origins, so a malformed `ANTHROPIC_BYPASS_URL` bypasses TO anthropic-direct rather than silently disabling the path; disabled only on same resolved origin or no key), `isBypassEligibleFailure`, and the SDK retry orchestrator `createMessageWithBypass`. Eligibility mirrors the proxy: connection failures and 5xx retry; a 4xx (rate-limit/bad-request), a deliberate caller-abort (the classifier's own 10s timeout — `APIUserAbortError`), and an already-aborted signal do NOT (a second attempt would just fail or race a dead signal). `credential-proxy.ts` now imports the shared status set, reachability predicate, and `resolveBypassTarget` in place of its inline copies, and also routes its own upstream/bypass `URL` construction through a shared `parseAnthropicUrlOrDefault` so a malformed `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL` / `ANTHROPIC_BYPASS_URL` degrades to anthropic-direct with a warn instead of throwing at startup — consistent with the classifier's no-throw handling rather than a raw `new URL` crash (the warn names the env var but omits its value per `coding-policy: no-secrets`, since an endpoint URL can embed a credential and the logger only redacts a narrow Telegram-token pattern); its 19 tests are unchanged and green. This is infra reliability, not correctness — #672 already made an outage safe; this removes the outage's reach into Stage 2 in the first place. 28 new unit tests in `src/anthropic-bypass.test.ts` (db-free) cover the decision + retry logic and the malformed-URL fallback, plus 4 classifier-level integration cases (5xx → bypass verdict, connection-error → bypass, 4xx → no bypass, both-fail → failed pass). Refs #671, #672, closes #675.

- Narrowed `runGateChain`'s per-gate catch (`src/gates/index.ts`) so it swallows ONLY explicit recoverable gate failures and rethrows every other exception per `coding-policy: error-handling` (#674). The chain previously converted arbitrary throws into a `{ decision: 'pass', failed: true }` record, so even unexpected custom/plain `Error`s were silently turned into gate outcomes. `runGateChain` now downgrades only `RecoverableGateError` (a new explicit marker for operational failures that should fail open) plus the pre-existing unregistered-gate path; any other throw — including `TypeError`/`ReferenceError`/`RangeError`/`SyntaxError` and plain `Error` — is logged with full gate context (`gateName`/`groupFolder`) and then propagates so the bug or unclassified failure surfaces loudly instead of degrading the chain to allow-all. Logging the context before the rethrow matters because the caller's catch (`src/group-queue.ts` `runForGroup`) logs only `{ groupJid, err }` — without the chain's own line the gate identity needed for triage would be lost. The propagated exception is contained by that per-group try/catch (retry/backoff, eventually the circuit breaker), so the blast radius is one group rather than the orchestrator. The deny-preservation behaviour (#671) is unchanged for explicit recoverable failures (still recorded `failed`). Tests now cover built-in defect propagation, plain-`Error` propagation, explicit `RecoverableGateError` downgrade, the gate-context propagate log, and router-level propagation vs. fail-open for the recoverable marker. Clearing the pre-existing `no-catch-all/no-catch-all` warning the OpenAI policy reviewer flagged on #672, which is where this was split out from. Refs #671, #672, closes #674.

- Reopened #589: the in-container hard-exit watchdog kept hard-killing the morning-brief mid-compose even after #590 broadened its idle budget to 90s, and the brief silently never reached chat (exit 0 → `task_run_logs.status='success'`, so the incomplete run was invisible). Root cause: the watchdog's idle clock advances only on SDK message events, and #590's in-flight `tool_use` re-arm covers only the window between a `tool_use` and its `tool_result` (tool execution) — NOT the window between a `tool_result` and the next assistant turn, where an LLM inference request is in flight. On 2026-06-11 the brief's final compose-and-send turn stalled there (the credential-proxy failed over to anthropic-direct mid-call), emitted zero SDK events for 90s, and the watchdog `process.exit(0)`'d before `send_message` fired. Bumping the budget again is whack-a-mole — no fixed idle threshold distinguishes "stuck SDK iterator" from "compose turn legitimately stalled on a network blip", and partial-message streaming wouldn't help (a network-layer stall generates zero tokens, so no partial events fire). Structural fix, two halves. (1) The in-container post-close watchdog is disabled for maintenance one-shot spawns (`shouldArmHardExitWatchdog(isMaintenanceSession)` in `container/agent-runner/src/hard-exit-watchdog.ts`, gated in `index.ts`): it was always a tighter, redundant duplicate of the host-side `MAINTENANCE_CONTAINER_TIMEOUT` (300s) inactivity bound, and for maintenance `_close` flips early (no follow-up IPC expected) so the post-close idle budget became the entire compose window. Deferring to the 300s host timer covers the proxy-failover case directly (the stalled call recovers well within 300s). Interactive/default sessions keep the watchdog unchanged — there `_close` means the conversation genuinely idled and a long post-close compose is not expected, so growing idleness is a real stuck-iterator signal; the per-skill `drain_timeout_ms` override and in-flight `tool_use` re-arm (#590) stay live for them. (2) A maintenance container reaped by the host inactivity timeout is now classified `killed`, not `success` (`src/container-runner.ts`). A healthy maintenance one-shot reaches a terminal result and exits naturally (`scheduleClose` → `_close`) within seconds, so hitting the inactivity timeout overwhelmingly means it was still working when reaped — streamed *preview* output is not a delivered result, which is exactly the assumption the old `hadStreamingOutput → success` check got wrong (stream markers include terminal ones, so the rare delivered-then-hung shape is over-reported as killed; precise terminal-result tracking is #682). The timeout handler now resolves `status:'killed'` (with an actionable reason) for maintenance sessions; interactive sessions keep the idle-cleanup `success` semantics. `ContainerOutput.status` and the `AgentResult` subset gain `'killed'`; `src/task-scheduler.ts` maps a terminal `'killed'` to `task_run_logs.status='killed'` with the reason in the error column (parallel to #496's force-close reclassification), so audits and the redelivery path see a non-success retriable run instead of a silent success. Tests: `shouldArmHardExitWatchdog` arm/skip; container-runner maintenance-reaped→`killed` plus an interactive idle-cleanup→`success` guard against over-reach; task-scheduler terminal-`killed`→`status='killed'`. The heartbeat Phase C recovery net (admin tile) that should redeliver an undelivered brief — and which also failed to fire on 2026-06-11 — is a separate follow-up keying on this newly-honest `killed` status, tracked in `jbaruch/nanoclaw-admin`. Refs #589, #590, #545, #461, #496.

- Fixed a Stage 2 gate fail-open that turned every Haiku-classifier outage into allow-all on non-strict groups (#671). The host-side `haiku-classifier` gate returns `pass` only when it cannot run (api-error / timeout / unparseable / no-client), and under the last-gate-wins combinator (`src/gates/index.ts`) that `pass` let a deterministic trigger `deny` fall through to chain-end fail-open `allow`. So whenever the classifier's egress failed — it builds its own `Anthropic` client against `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL` (`nanoclaw-litellm`) and so bypasses the credential-proxy's LiteLLM→Anthropic bypass that agent containers ride, observed dying on `Connection error` — every non-strict Stage 2 group with trigger patterns degraded to allow-all, spawning one container per untagged message and replying to people who never tagged the bot. The classifier now tags its four failure returns `failed: true`, and `runGateChain` preserves the most recent upstream advisory `deny` when a gate that runs after it fails (returns a failed `pass`, throws, or is unregistered) instead of fail-opening — a failed downstream gate can no longer be more permissive than a healthy "no". A healthy classifier never returns `pass`, so the documented healthy truth table is unchanged; strict groups (`requiresTrigger===true`) were never affected (their chain is `['trigger']` only). This is the gating-logic fix that makes an outage safe; giving the classifier the proxy's bypass so its egress reachability matches the containers' is tracked as a follow-up on #671. Refs #671.

- Fixed `register_group(requiresTrigger=true)` leaving the trigger gate fail-open until the next orchestrator restart (#670). `register_group`'s IPC payload carries only the `trigger` string, never a `triggerPatterns` config; `registerGroup` (`src/index.ts`) cached that raw object into the in-memory `registeredGroups` map while `setRegisteredGroup` serialized the keyword pattern correctly into the SQLite row. Because the gate path reads `triggerPatterns` straight from the in-memory registry and never re-reads the DB, the trigger gate saw "no trigger patterns configured" and fail-opened on every message (one container spawn per untagged message) until `loadState()` reloaded from disk — which is why the persisted row looked correct, the cause was invisible to static reading, and the `set_trigger` "workaround" only appeared to help (it cleared on the coincident restart; `updateGroupTrigger` strips `triggerPatterns` and carried the same latent bug). `registerGroup` now re-reads the synthesized patterns into the cache via a new `hydrateRegisteredGroupTriggerPatterns` helper, and `updateGroupTrigger` (`src/db.ts`) returns the persisted patterns too so the `set_trigger` path fixes the live gate, not just the row. Shipped in #673. (Entry added here alongside the sibling #671 gate fail-open fix — it was missing from Unreleased after #673 merged.) Refs #670.

- Added `claude-opus-4-8` model rows to the `nanoclaw-litellm` gateway config (`container/litellm/litellm.config.yaml`) — a required companion to #667. #667 bumped the orchestrator default to `claude-opus-4-8[1m]`, but the gateway's `model_list` only carried `claude-opus-4-7`/sonnet-4-6/haiku rows, so a `claude-opus-4-8` request to `:4000` returned `400 "Invalid model name"` (verified live). A 400 is not a 5xx, so the credential-proxy's Anthropic-direct bypass would NOT have fired — main-tier spawns would have errored the moment #667 deployed. Adds the `claude-opus-4-8` primary + `claude-opus-4-8-anthropic-direct` rows and the fallback mapping, mirroring the 4-7 pair (4-7 rows kept for any lingering references). Must deploy together with #667. Refs #613, #667.

- Bumped the default agent model Opus 4.7 → 4.8 (`DEFAULT_AGENT_MODEL` in `src/container-runner.ts`, `claude-opus-4-8[1m]`). 4.8 is the newest Opus and at least as capable; verified the agent-runner `query()` config carries no 4.7-specific hardcoding — it uses `thinking: { type: 'adaptive', display: 'summarized' }` and env-driven `xhigh` effort, both of which 4.8 takes (Anthropic ships 4.8 ~3× cheaper than 4.7). This sets the main-tier and hard-tier base to 4.8; the per-trust-tier routing (#665) and per-group/maintenance/per-task overrides still apply on top, so trusted/untrusted stay on Sonnet/Haiku. Caveat tracked in #668: `src/usage-log.ts` `PRICING` has no explicit `claude-opus-4-8` row, so cost telemetry resolves 4.8 to 4.7 pricing via the family-version fallback — if 4.8's published rates are lower, spend reporting will over-state main's cost (the safe direction) until the PRICING table gains a 4.8 row with authoritative rates. Refs #613, #668.

- Added per-trust-tier model routing for inbound spawns (#613 Stage 1 — Claude tier-down). Until now every tier defaulted to Opus (`claude-opus-4-7[1m]`) — including untrusted-tier chats and ~30 scheduled maintenance tasks, the single largest cost line item. `src/container-runner.ts` now derives a tier-keyed BASE model per spawn via `resolveTierBaseModel(isMain, trusted, globalDefault)`: **main** keeps the global default (Opus — quality floor for the operator's own chats), **trusted** routes to Sonnet 4.6 (`TRUSTED_TIER_MODEL`), **untrusted** to Haiku 4.5 (`UNTRUSTED_TIER_MODEL`). The base is passed as the `globalDefault` argument to the existing `resolveSessionAgentModel` ladder, so per-group / maintenance / per-task overrides still win on top unchanged — this is purely a cheaper default for tiers that don't need Opus. All same-family Claude SKUs, so the agent-runner's `query()` config (tuned for Opus, with `xhigh` effort gracefully degrading on Sonnet/Haiku) needs no change; the cheaper tiers carry no `[1m]` suffix (extended context reserved for main's long sessions). The per-spawn audit log gains `tier` + `tierBaseModel` fields for cost attribution. Deliberately scoped to a no-quality-bet, Claude-only tier-down: the originally-planned open-weight routing (gpt-oss-20b for trivial tasks, V4-Flash for inbound) was dropped from the near-term plan after a live gateway smoke-test showed gpt-oss-20b is a reasoning model that burns its token budget on thinking and returns empty answers (a silent failure inside the SDK tool loop), and that most cheap open-weight models rank poorly on agentic tool use — the savings come from Claude tier-down, not open-weight swap-out. Out of scope here (follow-ups): scheduled-task model selection (trivial → Haiku, substantive → Sonnet) via each skill's `agentModel:` frontmatter in the plugin repos; and the gated Kimi-K2.6-on-untrusted open-weight experiment. (The main Opus 4.7 → 4.8 swap that was listed here landed separately in #667.) Refs #613.

- Fixed `scripts/deploy.sh` force-killing the `nanoclaw-litellm` gateway on every deploy and then never bringing it back — leaving the #609 LiteLLM cost-migration silently inert. The deploy agent-close path (step 5) excluded the gateway with `grep -Ev '^nanoclaw(-litellm)?$'`, an anchored pattern written when the container was named `nanoclaw-litellm`. PR #626 moved the gateway into its own UGOS Pro compose project, which renames the container to `nanoclaw-litellm-nanoclaw-litellm-1` (project + service + index) — the `$` anchor no longer matched, so the gateway fell into the agent set, had no `/workspace/ipc/input` mount to receive the `_close` sentinel, and was force-killed after the 30s grace window. Because a manual/graceful stop suppresses `restart: always` until the next explicit start, and `deploy.sh` never (re)started the separate UGOS project, the gateway stayed down — observed dead for 13 days (2026-05-25 → 2026-06-07) while the orchestrator silently rode the credential-proxy's Anthropic-direct bypass (no outage, but the migration collected zero gateway data). Two-part fix: (1) the infra-container exclusion is extracted to `scripts/exclude-infra-containers.sh` — a single `awk '/^nanoclaw-/ && !/^nanoclaw-litellm-nanoclaw-litellm-[0-9]+$/'` pass that matches the gateway's full `project-service-index` compose container name (index left flexible for a recreate/scale bump) rather than a bare anchor or a loose `^nanoclaw-litellm` prefix — the anchor missed the compose-suffixed name, and a loose prefix would wrongly drop a per-group agent whose slug starts with `litellm` (e.g. `nanoclaw-litellm-fans`). It is now used at all three agent-close sites (the signal-set build plus the grace-poll and holdout-kill `currently_running` derivations, which previously used an even looser `grep -v '^nanoclaw$'`); (2) a new deploy step 8 idempotently `docker compose up -d`s the gateway from its UGOS-symlinked dir (`/volume1/docker/nanoclaw-litellm`, so the compose project name resolves to `nanoclaw-litellm`, not the repo dir basename) after the step-7 orchestrator restart, self-healing the gateway from any stop source on every deploy. The existing `scripts/test_deploy_agent_grep.py` contract test (CI step "Python tests (deploy agent-grep predicate)"), which extracts and runs deploy.sh's actual predicate, is updated to the real gateway container name and now pins the regression both ways: the gateway compose name is dropped across replica-index bumps, while a `litellm`-prefixed group agent survives. Refs #609.

- Added a post-`tessl update` verification step to `scripts/deploy.sh` (step 3c) that fails the deploy when a declared workspace tile did not actually materialize at the registry's latest version. `tessl update` (step 3) can exit 0 and print `✔ Updated N plugins` while silently leaving a tile's on-disk content at the prior version — or absent entirely — so the orchestrator goes on to mount stale or missing tile content into every agent container while the deploy reports a clean success. This is the failure mode `nanoclaw-host: post-merge-publish-watch` guards against, but on the install/consumer side rather than the publish side. The new step treats the materialized `tessl-workspace/.tessl/plugins/<owner>/<tile>/tile.json` version as ground truth and compares it against the registry latest from in-container `tessl tile info` (run inside the orchestrator so it shares step 3's tessl auth and registry view); on any mismatch — wrong version, or not materialized at all — it prints the offending tile(s) with their on-disk-vs-registry versions and hard-fails before the restart steps (4-7), leaving the running orchestrator untouched. Deliberately does not trust tessl's own `✔ Updated` summary line or `tessl outdated`'s "Current" column for this, since both were observed reporting up-to-date while the on-disk files had not advanced.

- Forward `YOUTUBE_API_KEY` into main/trusted containers (`CONTAINER_VARS` + `SECRET_CONTAINER_VARS` in `src/container-runner.ts`, `.env.example`). The admin tile's `youtube-comment-check` skill was migrated off the dead Composio MCP-discovery path to the native YouTube Data API v3 (`commentThreads.list` + `videos.list`) in `jbaruch/nanoclaw-admin#339` (shipped admin 0.1.401), because Composio's YouTube toolkit has no comment-threads tool. That skill reads `YOUTUBE_API_KEY` and fails closed (clear stderr, no cursor advance) when it's absent — this change forwards the key so the skill functions. Like the other Google API keys, it routes through the `--env-file` (not `-e`) so it stays off `ps`/`docker ps`. The reader (admin skill) shipped first and fails-loud until this lands, so there's no silent regression in the interim. `src/secret-env-file.test.ts` gains a `SECRET_CONTAINER_VARS.has('YOUTUBE_API_KEY')` assertion. Refs `jbaruch/nanoclaw-admin#339`.

- Unified the main (`is_main=1`) container's auto-memory store with the cross-container `/workspace/trusted` corpus (#658), ending a split-brain where two parallel typed-memory stores accumulated and the three maintenance skills each read a different subset. Before: the Claude Code harness wrote accumulated feedback / owner-profile memory to a per-group `data/sessions/<group>/shared-memory` dir (bound over `/home/node/.claude/projects/-workspace-group/memory`, issue #57), while deliberate `Write` calls landed in the shared `~/nanoclaw/trusted` dir (bound at `/workspace/trusted`, readable by every main+trusted container) — so `tessl__memory-hygiene` validated only `trusted/` (the 34 per-container files were never checked for dead refs / stale dates), `tessl__soul-searching` scanned only the per-container store (the 44 `trusted/` `feedback_*.md` files never surfaced as SOUL.md candidates), and the de-facto write target for in-session behavioral feedback was the corpus the maintenance skills don't touch. `src/container-runner.ts` now sources the auto-memory bind from `path.join(process.cwd(), 'trusted')` — the same host dir `/workspace/trusted` uses — when `isMain`, so harness auto-memory writes land directly in the canonical corpus and all three maintenance skills (plus the future `tessl__memory-enrich`, admin#341) operate on one store. Scope is main-only by design: the maintenance skills run only in the main/maintenance container against `/workspace/trusted`, so consolidating the trusted-but-non-main groups' per-group auto-memory (which no maintenance skill reads) buys nothing — those groups keep their per-group `shared-memory` dir, and the #57 per-session→shared migration loop is now exercised by the trusted-group tests. Untrusted stays excluded entirely: a shared writable owner-state dir is a prompt-injection vector (the existing `CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_AUTO_MEMORY` gate). Accepted side effect for the single-operator deployment: because trusted (non-main) containers can write `/workspace/trusted`, their content now becomes auto-injected into the main container's context (MEMORY.md is harness-loaded) — the same injection shape denied to untrusted, extended to trusted at a trust level the operator accepts. This change is the spawn-config re-point only; the one-shot data migration (merge the 34 per-container files into `trusted/`, reconcile duplicates / conflicts / stale-by-context via an operator-dispositioned cleanup digest, snapshot for rollback) and the downstream admin-tile path updates (`tessl__soul-searching` feedback path → `/workspace/trusted`, admin#341 reframe) are tracked separately and must complete before this deploys, or the main container would momentarily read a `trusted/` corpus missing its 34 operational-feedback files. `buildVolumeMounts` tests split accordingly: a new main-group case asserts the auto-memory mount resolves to the same host path as `/workspace/trusted`, while the trusted-group case retains the per-group `shared-memory` assertion. Refs #658.

- Added `sessionize_get_events(slugs: string[])` — a host-side batch MCP tool that fetches many Sessionize events in one call (#656). `check-cfps` Step 4 re-verifies every stored `open`/`approved` CFP against Sessionize, and the standalone `nightly-cfp-sync` agent issued that as one `mcp__nanoclaw__sessionize_get_event` call per slug, sequentially — 112 calls / one agent turn each (~5s × 112 ≈ 9 min) on the 2026-06-01 run, which drained at ~11 min mid-Step-7 before persisting any state (`0 of 137` open/approved re-written, `task_run_logs.result = null`, CFP tags left stale). This is the convergent root cause behind `jbaruch/nanoclaw#581` (cfp-sync empty result) and `jbaruch/nanoclaw-admin#334` (Confitura priority tag stuck stale because the run never completes to persist). A container-side batch script is impossible: `SESSIONIZE_EVENT_API_KEY` is host-only (not in `CONTAINER_VARS`, deliberately proxied so the key never enters agent containers, some of which run untrusted content), so the fix is a host-side plural tool. `src/ipc.ts` extracts two exported helpers from the singular `sessionize_get_event` case — `normalizeSessionizeEvent(event, slug)` (the raw-payload → flat-shape mapper, total over partial payloads so it never throws) now shared by both tools, and `fetchSessionizeEventsBatch(slugs, fetchEvent, concurrency)` (bounded-concurrency fan-out, default 10, that isolates a per-slug network/timeout/parse failure into a `{slug, error}` entry so one bad slug can't sink the batch). The new `sessionize_get_events` IPC case reuses the existing per-slug fetch + normalization, caps a single request at 500 slugs (the payload originates in a container), and writes one `{ data: [{slug, ...normalized} | {slug, error}, …] }` array via the same `scriptResultPath`/`requestId` result-file shape the singular tool uses. `container/agent-runner/src/ipc-mcp-stdio.ts` registers the tool alongside the singular one (same `https?://sessionize.com/` prefix-strip applied per slug, 120s result-poll to cover a large batch). Net effect for the consumer: 112 agent turns → 1 MCP call, deterministic, key stays host-side. The `check-cfps` Step 4 switch to one batched call is tracked separately in `jbaruch/nanoclaw-admin#336` and, per `nanoclaw-host: overlay-tile-authoring` reader-without-writer, must not ship before this host tool is merged + deployed to the NAS. 24 tests in `src/ipc.test.ts` cover `normalizeSessionizeEvent` (cfp_open future/past/missing, cfp_url fallback vs `cfpLink`, nested-field extraction, sparse-payload defaults) and `fetchSessionizeEventsBatch` (input order preserved across chunk boundaries, throwing-slug isolation, non-Error throw stringified, concurrency never exceeds the cap, empty input). Refs #581, closes #656.

- Fixed #651: on an empty assistant turn the agent-runner published the rendered input prompt back into chat as a bot reply, leaking the `<system-reminder>` envelope plus the next inbound user message. In passive groups (`requires_trigger=0`) where `rules/default-silence.md` tells the agent to "produce zero output", the turn ends with no `text` block and no `tool_use`, yet the Claude Agent SDK populates the `result` message's `result` field with the rendered prompt (`Human: <system-reminder>...<messages>...`) instead of empty text. `runQuery` forwarded that to the orchestrator, whose `decideAgentOutputAction` saw non-empty text with `chat_displayed` falsy and sent + stored it — producing two leaked bot messages in `telegram_dedy-bukhtyat` (`tg:-1003732298037`) on 2026-05-30T21:26–21:27Z carrying msgs 1810/1812. The agent-side `<internal>…</internal>` workaround only suppressed the leak by making the turn non-empty (changing what the SDK echoes), so the proper fix belongs in the runtime. `index.ts` now tracks a per-turn `assistantEmittedContent` flag (set on the first non-empty `text` block or any `tool_use`); the success branch routes through the new `shouldSuppressEchoedResult` predicate (`container/agent-runner/src/empty-turn-echo-suppression.ts`) and drops the echoed text — emitting a clean terminal `{status:'success', result:null}` — when the turn emitted nothing. Distinct from #461 (loop drains with no `result` event at all); this handles a `result` event that DID arrive carrying the echo. Closes #651.

- Fixed the #544 skill-dependency closure missing every `Skill(skill: "X", args: "...")` invocation, which silently defeated the rescue logic it was built for. `SKILL_INVOCATION_PATTERN` in `src/skill-dep-closure.ts` ended with `["']\s*\)` — requiring the closing paren immediately after the skill name's closing quote — so the args-bearing form never matched and the referenced skill was invisible to the closure. The maintenance blocklist therefore treated the dependency as unreachable and removed the dependee's prompt, producing `Unknown skill: <X>` at agent runtime. This is the exact failure #544 shipped to prevent: on 2026-05-31 the maintenance container fired `cadence-registry::telegram_swarm::tessl__wiki-lint` and reported `Wiki lint failed: Skill(wiki) returned error — Unknown skill: wiki` because `wiki-lint/SKILL.md` invokes `Skill(skill: "wiki", args: "lint")` and `builtin/wiki` was on the blocklist. Blast radius was broader than wiki-lint — every skill invoking a sub-skill with `args:` (e.g. `morning-brief` → `tessl__resumable-cycle`) was equally invisible and would have crashed the day its target landed on a blocklist. The regex now ends with `["'][^)]*\)`: `[^)]*` skips any trailing named params up to the closing paren, and since the skill name is already captured before it, the captured group is unaffected by whatever follows. Added `extractSkillDeps` cases for the args form (bare and `tessl__`-prefixed, mixed with no-args invocations) plus a closure-level test reproducing the wiki-lint → wiki outage with the args form. Refs #544, closes #652.

- Fixed `scripts/deploy.sh` silently reporting a successful deploy when `tessl update` actually failed. Step 3 piped the update through `… 2>&1 | tail -10`, which both truncated the error and **discarded tessl's exit code** (a shell pipeline returns the last command's status, so `tail`'s `0` always won). When `tessl update` failed — most often because the tessl CLI auto-updates its own binary in the background (`~/.tessl/auto-update.log`; `0.80 → 0.81` observed) and that swap races the deploy's update — the orchestrator kept serving stale tiles while the deploy printed "Deploy complete". Step 3 now runs `tessl update` without the masking pipe (full output preserved), checks the real exit status, retries once after a 15s settle to ride out the binary-swap race, and **hard-fails the deploy** (exit 1) if the update still fails — so a broken tile sync can never masquerade as success. `--accept-warnings` still installs tiles carrying advisory moderation verdicts (e.g. a documented `.env.example` flagged `W008`/`E006` — false positives on placeholder secret names), so those do not fail the deploy; only a non-zero `tessl update` does. Caught 2026-05-29 when a deploy reported success but the orchestrator stayed on the prior `nanoclaw-admin` version and `nanoclaw-flight-assist` never received its precheck-timeout fix — the failure was invisible until the installed tile version was checked by hand.

- Restored `mcp__composio__*` tools for unattended agent containers after Composio moved its consumer "Connect" MCP gateway (`connect.composio.dev/mcp`) from `x-consumer-api-key` header auth to interactive AuthKit-JWT OAuth. The agent runner registered that gateway with the `ck_*` `COMPOSIO_MCP_KEY`; once Composio flipped the auth model (~2026-05-23) every request 401'd with `No Authorization: Bearer header`, silently disabling Gmail/Calendar/Tasks tools inside maintenance containers. The headless `check-orders` and `check-email` skills — which route all Gmail through `COMPOSIO_REMOTE_WORKBENCH` (Composio's cloud sandbox where the poison-defense sanitizer strips invisible-Unicode/injection from raw bodies before they reach the agent) — could no longer fetch; this surfaced as `nanoclaw#581`'s recurring `nightly-external-sync exited step-7: inner-skill-fail`. OAuth is a non-starter for scheduled containers (no browser, no human at fire time; a one-time authorize only lasts until the refresh token expires, then breaks again unattended). Composio's REST gateway (`backend.composio.dev/api/v3`, `x-api-key`, project-scoped `ak_*` key) was never affected — `tessl__composio-fetch`'s precheck kept fetching Gmail/Calendar fine throughout.
